PMID 42029096 (2026) reports that B. microti vesicles trigger NETosis and cytokine release in mouse neutrophils in vitro; this is murine, in vitro, and was not used as a human comparator. | 1) Readouts differ: canine MPXI (cytochemical index from an analyser) versus human granule-gene transcripts (sepsis) and plasma granule proteins (malaria); the canine measure does not distinguish activation from degranulation or from increased granulocyte maturity. 2) Feintuch compares two severe malaria phenotypes, so the neutrophil signal marks cerebral sequestration vasculopathy, not death or severity versus mild disease; sequestration biology is specific to P. falciparum. 3) Sepsis is mostly bacterial and adult, with ICU treatment, vasopressors and organ support; canine cohorts are untreated or recently treated infections with different baseline neutrophil biology. 4) Canine cohorts (CB050) include no mechanistic neutrophil assays, and no study shows a neutrophil-driven pathway in canine babesiosis or in human babesiosis (neutropenia is described in human babesiosis, PMID 26071466, but not mechanistically). 5) Giannini adjusted for neutrophil count; CB050 did not adjust for count or maturity. | **mechanistically plausible** — Both hosts show a neutrophil-activation signal linked to worse outcome, but through non-equivalent measurements (MPXI in dogs; granule transcripts and plasma proteins in humans) and in different diseases; no neutrophil granule protein, NET or degranulation measure exists in dogs or in human babesiosis, so the pathway is plausible rather than shown in both. |
| INF | Interferon/regulatory counter-response (IFN-gamma, SOCS3, IL-10, type I interferon) balancing or failing to restrain inflammation in severe disease | [CB109](articles/CB109_2023_haematological-indices-immune-response-profiles-naturally.md) (B. canis, n=32 babesiosis in gene-expression subset with 3 healthy controls): highest IFN-gamma and SOCS3 expression in severe babesiosis. [CB082](articles/CB082_2021_experimental-babesia-rossi-infection-induces-hemolytic.md) (experimental B. rossi, n=5): interferon/antiviral pathway responses in whole-blood RNA-seq. [CB075](articles/CB075_2021_prevalence-genetic-biochemical-evaluation-immune-response.md) (B. vogeli, subgroups of about 4-5 dogs): higher IFN-gamma and TNF-alpha than healthy dogs. [CB008](articles/CB008_2017_flow-cytometric-assessment-lymphocyte-immunophenotypes-naturally.md) (B. rossi, n=44): complicated cases had lower CD3+ and CD4+ percentages, read as immune suppression. No canine IL-10 or type I interferon severity data appear in the INF digest. | PMID 36759905 ([HU004](articles/HU004_2023_elevated-plasma-interleukin-8-risk-factor.md)): IL-10 (anti-inflammatory), CXCL9 (IFN-gamma-induced) and granzyme B were higher in fatal paediatric cerebral malaria, consistent with an IFN-gamma axis accompanied by a compensatory regulatory signal. PMID 26884431 ([HU005](articles/HU005_2016_activated-neutrophils-are-associated-pediatric-cerebral.md)): Ret-CM children (no cerebral sequestration) had lower inflammation, higher alpha interferon and Toll-like receptor pathway upregulation than Ret+CM. | Canine IFN-gamma/SOCS3 data are tiny and mRNA-based (CB109) or from very small groups (CB075); human data are plasma proteins and transcriptomes; cause and effect are unknown in both; the human interferon-alpha signal is linked to absence of sequestration in malaria, a feature not shown for Babesia. Co-infections (E. canis in CB075) are a confounder in the canine data. | **speculative** — Both hosts show interferon-axis and regulatory-cytokine changes in severe disease, but the canine evidence is small, partly mRNA-only, and not paired with an IL-10 or interferon-alpha comparison, and direction relative to protection versus pathology is unresolved. |
| END | Systemic endothelial activation shown by soluble endothelial markers (adhesion molecules, thrombomodulin) that rise with infection and relate to organ dysfunction | [CB004](articles/CB004_2017_blood-markers-fibrinolysis-endothelial-activation-babesiosis.md) (2017, B. canis, n=30 infected dogs, 11 complicated and 19 uncomplicated, vs 10 healthy controls): thrombomodulin, HMGB-1, VCAM-1 and suPAR were raised versus healthy dogs and normalised or shifted by day 6. [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md) (2026, B. rossi, n=97 infected, 12 non-survivors, 15 controls): thromboinflammation with platelet activation, fibrinolytic shutdown and fibrin microthrombi at necropsy. [CB087](articles/CB087_2021_morphofunctional-changes-spleen-components-pathogenetic-substantiation.md) (2021, n=187 dogs): endothelial desquamation in spleens (descriptive). | PMID 29872039 ([HU010](articles/HU010_2018_intravascular-haemolysis-severe-plasmodium-knowlesi-malaria.md)): ICAM-1, E-selectin, Ang-2 and osteoprotegerin rose with severity in P. knowlesi malaria (48 severe, 154 non-severe, 50 controls), and osteoprotegerin was independently linked to ICAM-1 and E-selectin. PMID 33541396 ([HU012](articles/HU012_2021_angiopoietin-2-outperforms-other-endothelial-biomarkers.md)): Ang-2, sVE-cadherin, endocan and syndecan-1 were all higher in septic patients with severe AKI (n=228). | The analytes overlap only at the class level (VCAM-1 and thrombomodulin in dogs; ICAM-1, E-selectin and Ang-2 in humans). The canine cohorts are small, with 10-15 controls, and CB004 and CB145 are associational. Dogs were sampled at admission with variable treatment and the digest does not show a marker-to-outcome link in CB004. Human assays are validated for human proteins and canine cross-reactivity is a separate issue. Sepsis has no parasite, and P. knowlesi is not a sequestering parasite to the degree P. falciparum is. | **directly demonstrated in both** — Endothelial activation by soluble markers was measured in both hosts (CB004 in B. canis; PMIDs 29872039 and 33541396 in humans). The claim is limited to the generic phenomenon, because the specific markers differ and the dog data are associational. It does not extend to the Ang-2/Tie2 axis. |
| END | Angiopoietin-2 release and Tie2 destabilisation of the endothelium, with Ang-2 as an independent predictor of organ injury (AKI) | No canine study in the END digest measured angiopoietin-2, angiopoietin-1 or Tie2. The closest data are [CB004](articles/CB004_2017_blood-markers-fibrinolysis-endothelial-activation-babesiosis.md) (thrombomodulin, VCAM-1 and HMGB-1 raised, n=30 vs 10 controls), [CB122](articles/CB122_2024_characterization-lc-ms-ms-based-proteomic.md) (serum extracellular vesicle proteins from B. canis dogs enriched for adhesion and VEGF pathways, preliminary, 15 vs 15), and [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md) (thromboinflammation, 97 dogs). | PMID 29872039 ([HU010](articles/HU010_2018_intravascular-haemolysis-severe-plasmodium-knowlesi-malaria.md)): Ang-2 rose with severity in P. knowlesi malaria and was an independent risk factor for AKI (AKI in 88% of severe cases). PMID 33541396 ([HU012](articles/HU012_2021_angiopoietin-2-outperforms-other-endothelial-biomarkers.md)): Ang-2 was the only endothelial marker independently associated with severe AKI in sepsis (OR 6.07 per log increase, 95% CI 2.34-15.78). PMID 30753363 ([HU011](articles/HU011_2019_glycocalyx-breakdown-associated-severe-disease-fatal.md)): glycocalyx breakdown in falciparum malaria correlated with parasite biomass. | Ang-2 has not been measured in canine babesiosis, and a validated canine Ang-2 assay would be needed. Ang-2 is a non-specific marker of critical illness and also rises in non-infectious conditions, so rising Ang-2 in dogs would not by itself indicate a shared mechanism. Canine babesiosis AKI patterns differ (CB044 describes azotaemic and non-azotaemic dogs, hypotension and SIADH-like changes, not an endothelial AKI model). | **mechanistically plausible** — Endothelial activation is demonstrated in dogs (CB004), and Ang-2 is a Weibel-Palade-body product and a generic downstream marker in human malaria and sepsis. However, no canine Ang-2 or Tie2 data exist in the digest, so the same mechanism has not been measured in both hosts. |
| END | Endothelial glycocalyx degradation (syndecan-1 shedding, urinary glycosaminoglycans) with vascular leak and organ dysfunction | No canine study in the END digest measured syndecan-1, heparan sulfate, urinary GAGs or glycocalyx imaging. Indirect, non-specific findings only: [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) (2021, n=6 fatal B. canis) multi-organ congestion, oedema, effusions and proposed circulatory failure; [CB146](articles/CB146_2026_cardiac-involvement-european-babesiosis-pathological-perspective.md) (2026) cardiac haemorrhage, oedema, congestion and effusion; [CB111](articles/CB111_2023_lung-pathology-natural-babesia-rossi-infection.md) (2023, B. rossi) alveolar oedema with exudative diffuse alveolar damage overlap; [CB087](articles/CB087_2021_morphofunctional-changes-spleen-components-pathogenetic-substantiation.md) endothelial desquamation in the spleen. | PMID 30753363 ([HU011](articles/HU011_2019_glycocalyx-breakdown-associated-severe-disease-fatal.md)): urinary GAGs were highest in severe falciparum malaria (n=129; dye assay mean 3.98 severe vs 0.22 controls), higher in 3 deaths than 39 survivors, and inversely related to NO bioavailability. PMID 33541396 ([HU012](articles/HU012_2021_angiopoietin-2-outperforms-other-endothelial-biomarkers.md)): syndecan-1 was higher in septic patients with severe AKI but was not independent of Ang-2 (n=228). | Oedema, effusions and congestion in dogs have many causes (hypoxia, hypoalbuminaemia, anaemia, inflammation, DIC) and the digest contains no glycocalyx-specific measurement. Syndecan-1 shedding in sepsis is heavily confounded by shock and fluid resuscitation. The human malaria data come from adults in endemic settings with different parasite biology. | **speculative** — Only non-specific pathology (oedema, congestion, endothelial desquamation) links the hosts. Glycocalyx degradation was measured in humans (PMID 30753363) but not in dogs in the digest, and shared clinical or histological signs alone are insufficient. |
| END | Haemolysis-driven loss of nitric oxide bioavailability (cell-free haemoglobin) causing endothelial dysfunction and impaired microvascular reactivity | No canine study in the END digest measured nitric oxide, ADMA, cell-free haemoglobin or flow-mediated vascular reactivity. Haemolytic anaemia is a hallmark of canine babesiosis across the digest (e.g. [CB099](articles/CB099_2022_occurrence-babesiosis-rhine-main-area-hesse.md): 82% anaemic and 90% hyperbilirubinaemic of 81 PCR-positive dogs). [CB107](articles/CB107_2023_pathogenesis-anemia-babesiosis-possible-contribution-pro.md) (review) attributes canine babesial anaemia mainly to host immune responses, and [CB006](articles/CB006_2017_relation-antioxidant-status-admission-disease-severity.md) reports oxidative imbalance (lower SOD, GPx, catalase; higher MDA) associated with MODS and death in 40 dogs. | PMID 29872039 ([HU010](articles/HU010_2018_intravascular-haemolysis-severe-plasmodium-knowlesi-malaria.md)): cell-free haemoglobin rose with severity in P. knowlesi malaria and was linked to Ang-2 and osteoprotegerin, and osteoprotegerin was independently associated with impaired nitric oxide-dependent endothelial function and microvascular reactivity. PMID 30753363 ([HU011](articles/HU011_2019_glycocalyx-breakdown-associated-severe-disease-fatal.md)): glycocalyx breakdown was inversely linked to endothelial NO bioavailability in falciparum malaria. | Intravascular versus extravascular haemolysis proportions differ by Babesia species and by host, and splenic clearance matters (CB021: splenectomised B. vogeli dogs become severely anaemic and thrombocytopenic). NO bioavailability was measured functionally in humans (reactive hyperaemia-type testing) and has no canine equivalent in the digest. Oxidative-stress data in dogs (CB006) are not NO-specific. | **speculative** — Haemolysis is common to both diseases but the NO-scavenging step was measured only in human malaria (PMIDs 29872039 and 30753363). The dog evidence is limited to anaemia and generic oxidative stress markers, which are shared clinical signs rather than the same mechanism. |
| END | Microvascular obstruction and dysfunction (parasitised-cell sequestration, thromboinflammation, impaired microvascular reactivity) leading to hypoperfusion and organ failure | [CB041](articles/CB041_2019_mechanisms-involved-persistence-babesia-canis-infection.md) (2019, review of the author's own experimental B. canis model): proposes capillary sequestration of infected erythrocytes via endothelial attachment, consolidated by coagulation-mediated trapping, to explain severe disease despite low circulating parasitaemia. [CB010](articles/CB010_2017_genome-wide-analysis-gene-expression-protein.md) (2017): predicted B. canis exportome includes 103 VESA models (candidate cytoadhesion ligands; not functionally demonstrated here). [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md): fibrin microthrombi at necropsy in 12 non-survivors. [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) (n=6) and [CB146](articles/CB146_2026_cardiac-involvement-european-babesiosis-pathological-perspective.md): congestion, haemorrhage, leukostasis and proposed circulatory failure. No in vivo canine microcirculation imaging is in the digest. | PMID 29872039 ([HU010](articles/HU010_2018_intravascular-haemolysis-severe-plasmodium-knowlesi-malaria.md)): impaired microvascular reactivity in severe knowlesi malaria, associated with endothelial activation and AKI. PMID 30753363 ([HU011](articles/HU011_2019_glycocalyx-breakdown-associated-severe-disease-fatal.md)): glycocalyx degradation tied to parasite biomass, severity and death in falciparum malaria. PMID 33541396 ([HU012](articles/HU012_2021_angiopoietin-2-outperforms-other-endothelial-biomarkers.md)): endothelial injury (Ang-2) linked to circulatory and coagulation organ failure in sepsis. | P. falciparum sequestration is mediated by PfEMP1 and is well characterised, whereas Babesia sequestration in dogs rests largely on one research group's model and a narrative review (CB041), and has not been shown by in vivo imaging. Knowlesi malaria shows severe disease with limited sequestration, so its microvascular dysfunction may be haemolysis and inflammation driven, which is the closer analogy to canine disease. Sepsis microcirculatory failure is not parasite-driven. Canine necropsy findings are end-stage and cannot show temporal sequence. | **mechanistically plausible** — Canine histology (microthrombi, congestion, leukostasis) and the sequestration hypothesis are consistent with human microvascular dysfunction, but microcirculatory function itself was measured only in humans (PMID 29872039). The canine side is hypothesis and necropsy, not a direct measurement of the same variable. |
| COA | Consumptive coagulopathy and thrombocytopenia progressing to DIC in severe haemoprotozoal infection (platelet consumption, antithrombin loss, fibrinolytic shutdown, microthrombi) | [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md) (B. rossi, 97 dogs vs 15 controls, 12 non-survivors with necropsy): severe thrombocytopenia, platelet activation, factor consumption, fibrinolytic shutdown and fibrin microthrombi with high CRP/SAA, i.e. a thromboinflammatory phenotype. [CB011](articles/CB011_2017_alteration-haemostatic-parameters-uncomplicated-babesiosis.md) (B. canis, n=25): antithrombin III decreased and TAT raised. [CB034](articles/CB034_2019_thromboelastographic-platelet-mapping-complicated-babesia-rossi.md) (B. rossi, n=13): higher fibrinogen and fibrin clot strength offsetting low platelets on thromboelastography. [CB079](articles/CB079_2021_multiple-complications-infected-babesia-gibsoni.md) (B. gibsoni, n=1) and [CB005](articles/CB005_2017_prevalence-impact-babesia-canis-theileria-sp.md) (captive wolf) report DIC. Thrombocytopenia is near-universal across [CB022](articles/CB022_2018_haemato-biochemical-changes-infected-babesia-sp.md), [CB048](articles/CB048_2020_molecular-detection-hematological-changes-naturally-infected.md), [CB056](articles/CB056_2020_babesiosis-latvian-domestic-2016-2019.md), [CB064](articles/CB064_2020_hematological-profile-blood-parasitic-infected-southern.md), [CB078](articles/CB078_2021_hematological-alterations-large-babesia-species-infection.md), [CB131](articles/CB131_2025_molecular-detection-hematological-alterations-infected-babesia.md). | PMID 11283800 ([HU007](articles/HU007_2001_severe-babesiosis-long-island-review-34.md)) (Hatcher 2001, n=34 severe human babesiosis): thrombocytopenia more frequent than in febrile controls, and 41% had complications including DIC, associated with severe anaemia and parasitaemia above 10% (borderline). PMID 31162936 ([HU008](articles/HU008_2019_novel-assay-neutrophil-extracellular-trap-formation.md)) is a sepsis comparator, not babesiosis. No coagulation assay data in human babesiosis were found in the retrieved literature, and recent human babesiosis hits were case reports (e.g. splenic infarction, thrombocytopenia, DIC with HLH) or administrative cohorts such as PMID 41666444 (TriNetX babesiosis sepsis, n=1,566, ICD-code based). | The canine data measure mechanism (TAT, antithrombin, fibrinolysis, TEG, necropsy microthrombi). The human babesiosis data are clinical counts only, with no mechanism and no DIC definition in the abstract. Different species: B. rossi, B. canis and B. gibsoni versus mainly B. microti in the US (Hatcher; species not stated in abstract). The human series is from severe, hospitalised, often asplenic or older patients, whereas the canine cohorts include uncomplicated cases. Treatment and supportive care differ. B. rossi dogs show hyperfibrinogenaemia and fibrinolytic shutdown that has not been tested in humans. | **mechanistically plausible** — Thrombocytopenia and DIC occur clinically in both hosts, but only the canine studies measure the consumptive and fibrinolytic mechanism. Human babesiosis lacks the same measurements, so overlap rests on clinical syndrome plus the shared biology of severe haemolytic parasitaemia. |
| COA | Neutrophil extracellular traps (NETs) as a driver of immunothrombosis and DIC | No NET, citrullinated histone or cell-free DNA measurement appears in the COA or COA (secondary) digest entries. The nearest canine data are [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md) (thromboinflammation: platelet activation, fibrin microthrombi, high CRP/SAA, 97 dogs) and [CB004](articles/CB004_2017_blood-markers-fibrinolysis-endothelial-activation-babesiosis.md) (HMGB-1 raised in 30 B. canis dogs, a damage-associated molecular pattern that can induce NETs, but NETs were not measured). [CB078](articles/CB078_2021_hematological-alterations-large-babesia-species-infection.md) notes neutrophils low or normal in large Babesia infections. | PMID 31162936 ([HU008](articles/HU008_2019_novel-assay-neutrophil-extracellular-trap-formation.md)) (Abrams 2019, 341 ICU patients, mostly sepsis for strong NET responders): plasma NET-forming capacity independently predicted DIC and 28-day mortality after APACHE II adjustment, and IL-8 was a driver. This is sepsis, not babesiosis. A malaria NET cohort (PMID 30452670, Kho 2019, severe falciparum n=8) found NETs rising with severity but its severe group is very small and the record carries an erratum (PMID 30768162, content not inspected). No human babesiosis NET study was found. | NETs were not measured in canine or human babesiosis in the retrieved material, so the link is carried entirely by sepsis. Abrams measures plasma NET-inducing capacity ex vivo, not circulating NETs in tissue. Sepsis is bacteria-driven and IL-8 dependent; babesiosis is intraerythrocytic with haemolysis and free haem, which can also trigger NETs in malaria models but is untested here. Dog neutrophil NET biology (canine NETs exist in vitro) has not been linked to babesiosis outcomes in the digest. | **speculative** — No study in either host measures NETs in babesiosis. The connection is an inference from sepsis-DIC data and from thromboinflammation markers (CB145, CB004) that do not specifically implicate NETs. |
| COA | Endothelial activation with microvascular thrombosis and dysregulation of the VWF-ADAMTS13 axis in severe haemoprotozoal disease | [CB004](articles/CB004_2017_blood-markers-fibrinolysis-endothelial-activation-babesiosis.md) (B. canis, 30 dogs vs 10 controls; 11 complicated): thrombomodulin, VCAM-1, HMGB-1 and suPAR raised, indicating endothelial activation and altered fibrinolysis, shifting or normalising by day 6. [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md): fibrin microthrombi at necropsy in non-survivors. [CB041](articles/CB041_2019_mechanisms-involved-persistence-babesia-canis-infection.md) (narrative review): proposed capillary sequestration of infected erythrocytes via endothelial attachment, consolidated by coagulation. VWF and ADAMTS13 were not measured in any COA digest entry. | PMID 19300493 ([HU009](articles/HU009_2009_severe-plasmodium-falciparum-malaria-associated-circulating.md)) (Larkin 2009, severe n=20, cerebral n=13 falciparum): raised VWF antigen, disproportionately raised VWF collagen-binding, abnormal ultra-large multimers, ADAMTS13 activity about 55% of normal and plasma inhibition of ADAMTS13. Supporting context seen but not selected: PMID 26830467 (Ugandan children, VWF antigen associated with mortality) and PMID 40283058 (2025 meta-analysis: VWF higher in malaria than controls, SMD 2.689, 12 studies, 3,109 participants, but no significant severe versus non-severe difference, I2 above 98%). | Different markers: canine studies measured thrombomodulin, VCAM-1, suPAR, whereas the human data measured VWF and ADAMTS13; endothelial activation is shared but the specific VWF-ADAMTS13 mechanism is not shown in dogs. P. falciparum sequesters via PfEMP1 binding to ICAM-1 and EPCR in brain and organ microvasculature; sequestration in B. canis is only proposed (CB041) and not shown to depend on a comparable adhesin. Larkin is a single-timepoint Ghanaian study with an unidentified inhibitor; the 2025 meta-analysis shows heterogeneous severity effects. Splenic status matters: CB021 shows B. vogeli is severe mainly in splenectomised dogs, a host factor that also shapes human babesiosis. | **mechanistically plausible** — Endothelial activation with microthrombosis is demonstrated in canine babesiosis (CB004, CB145) and in severe malaria (Larkin), but the VWF-ADAMTS13 imbalance itself has been measured only in malaria. Biological plausibility is supported but not demonstrated in babesiosis. |
| REN | Early proximal tubular injury detectable in urine (NGAL, KIM-1) before or without a creatinine rise, in a haemoparasitic infection | [CB024](articles/CB024_2018_glomerular-tubular-kidney-damage-markers-babesiosis.md) (B. canis, n=42 infected vs 14 controls): urinary KIM-1, albumin, IgG, RBP and NAG raised, correlated with UPC, including in non-azotaemic dogs. [CB103](articles/CB103_2022_serum-urine-profiling-high-throughput-tmt.md) (B. canis, n=22 vs 8 controls, TMT proteomics): urinary NGAL and L-FABP rose before azotaemia. [CB124](articles/CB124_2024_diagnostic-efficacy-urinary-neutrophil-gelatinase-associated.md) (babesiosis and leptospirosis pooled): urinary NGAL and KIM-1 raised already in IRIS grade I AKI. [CB053](articles/CB053_2020_evaluation-acute-kidney-injury-complicated-or.md) (B. rossi, n=35 vs 10): tubular and glomerular markers raised and normalised by one month while creatinine/SDMA were unchanged. [CB001](articles/CB001_2017_utility-urinary-markers-assessment-renal-dysfunction.md) (B. canis, n=10 vs 10): urinary RBP and Tamm-Horsfall protein raised. All associational, small natural-infection cohorts. | PMID 27612570 ([HU013](articles/HU013_2016_neutrophil-gelatinase-associated-lipocalin-ngal-predicts.md)): in 39 imported falciparum patients, urine NGAL (AUROC 1.00), serum NGAL and urine KIM-1 predicted AKI (6 events). Supporting alternates found but not selected as main papers: PMID 31966633 (autopsy kidneys, n=10 AKI/10 non-AKI/10 normal: proximal tubular KIM-1 and MMP-3 strongly raised in malarial AKI, correlated with creatinine and tubular necrosis) and PMID 40360328 (Indian severe falciparum cohort: plasma NGAL strongly associated with AKI severity). No human babesiosis study of NGAL/KIM-1 was found. PMID 39873391 ([HU014](articles/HU014_2025_comparative-outcomes-babesiosis-immunocompromised-nonimmunocompromised-hosts.md)) reports AKI incidence in human babesiosis but no biomarkers. | Human evidence is from P. falciparum, not Babesia; no human babesiosis tubular-biomarker study was identified. Malaria involves cytoadherent sequestration, which Babesia lacks in the same form (B. rossi microvascular pathology in dogs is a partial analogue). Human NGAL study is a 39-patient pilot of non-immune travellers with only 6 AKI cases, so AUROC 1.00 is likely optimistic; canine cohorts are also small and define AKI by IRIS/creatinine/SDMA rather than KDIGO. Canine work measures urinary markers against healthy controls, not prediction of later AKI, and urinary NGAL/KIM-1 assays are species-specific. Dogs have recurring co-pathologies (glomerular injury, proteinuria) that human malaria papers measure less. | **directly demonstrated in both** — The same readouts (urinary NGAL and KIM-1 as markers of tubular injury preceding or accompanying creatinine rise) are measured in dogs with babesiosis and in humans with falciparum malaria, with concordant direction. The rating applies to the dog-to-malaria comparison; it is not demonstrated in human babesiosis, and the human sample is small, so conclusions should be limited to biomarker behaviour, not shared aetiology. |
| REN | Intravascular haemolysis releasing cell-free haemoglobin, driving oxidative (lipid-peroxidation) injury to renal tubules | No REN-category canine study in the digest measured cell-free haemoglobin, haemoglobinuria or F2-isoprostanes against kidney outcome. Indirect only: [CB083](articles/CB083_2021_molecular-characterization-assessment-hematobiochemical-oxidative-indices.md) (B. vogeli, natural infection) reported higher MDA, lower SOD and raised creatinine/urea versus uninfected dogs, but these are group-level co-findings and the digest does not state a linkage to AKI. [CB154](articles/CB154_2026_comprehensive-assessment-babesia-gibsoni-infection-clinicopathological.md) (B. gibsoni, Mizoram) found higher LPO and lower GSH/T-AOC with multiorgan changes; LPO correlated with RBC count, not shown with renal indices. [CB142](articles/CB142_2025_effect-n-acetylcysteine-oxidative-stress-hematological.md) (B. gibsoni, 12 enrolled) found NAC adjunct improved antioxidant capacity only. [CB097](articles/CB097_2022_pathomorphological-changes-german-boxer-infected-babesia.md)/CB080 necropsies showed haemosiderophage accumulation and congestion without kidney-specific mechanism. [CB096](articles/CB096_2022_sirs-mods-imha-associated-babesia-canis.md) reports Coombs-positive haemolysis with azotaemia in one pup. | PMID 28449641 ([HU002](articles/HU002_2017_cell-free-hemoglobin-mediated-oxidative-stress.md)): in 107 severe falciparum malaria patients (58% AKI), plasma cell-free haemoglobin, F2-isoprostanes and isofurans were higher with AKI, correlated with haemolysis and parasite burden, independently associated with rising creatinine over 72 h, and F2-isoprostanes/isofurans/deformability were associated with later dialysis. A sepsis group (n=28) was included as a comparator. Supporting alternate: PMID 29872039 ([HU010](articles/HU010_2018_intravascular-haemolysis-severe-plasmodium-knowlesi-malaria.md)) (P. knowlesi, n=48 severe/154 non-severe: CFHb rose with severity; AKI in 88% of severe cases; haemolysis-linked endothelial activation markers angiopoietin-2 and osteoprotegerin were independent AKI risk factors). For human babesiosis, PMID 39873391 ([HU014](articles/HU014_2025_comparative-outcomes-babesiosis-immunocompromised-nonimmunocompromised-hosts.md)) gives AKI rates and higher parasitaemia in ICAH patients but no CFH or oxidative-stress data. | Canine babesiosis haemolysis includes an immune-mediated component (e.g. Coombs-positive cases such as CB096) and, in B. rossi, haemolysis that is not strictly proportional to parasitaemia; malaria haemolysis is parasite-burden-linked with pitting and red cell rigidity. Malaria AKI studies have a sepsis comparator and serial creatinine; canine studies lack CFH and use cross-sectional sampling. Haemoglobinuria is a recognised feature in some canine babesiosis (not quantified in digest REN entries) but is not measured as exposure in any listed canine study. P. falciparum sequestration contributes microvascular obstruction absent in B. microti. Antioxidant markers in dogs are systemic and not tied to renal outcome. | **mechanistically plausible** — Human data directly link CFH and lipid peroxidation to AKI in malaria. Canine babesiosis shows haemolysis, oxidative-stress shifts and tubular injury biomarkers, but no canine study in the digest measured CFH or tested the CFH-to-tubular-injury pathway. The link is therefore inferred, not demonstrated in dogs or in human babesiosis. |
| REN | AKI as a correlate of high-severity babesiosis with multi-organ dysfunction (ARDS) and worse outcome, with host risk factors for severe disease | [CB025](articles/CB025_2018_renal-azotemia-associated-clinical-laboratory-findings.md) (B. rossi, n=152 records): renal azotaemia in 14% (21/152), associated with collapse, hypoglycaemia, cerebral babesiosis, ARDS and lower survival; USG <1.030 missed most. [CB061](articles/CB061_2020_complications-risk-factors-regarding-outcomes-babesiosis.md) (B. canis, n=240): creatinine >5 mg/dL associated with death; mortality 33.9% with renal involvement. [CB119](articles/CB119_2023_diverse-pathogenicity-various-babesia-parasite-species.md) (narrative review): B. rossi ranked most pathogenic, B. gibsoni and B. vogeli less so. Association only; none relates renal injury to parasitaemia or to splenic status. | PMID 39873391 ([HU014](articles/HU014_2025_comparative-outcomes-babesiosis-immunocompromised-nonimmunocompromised-hosts.md)) (n=225): AKI 24% in immunocompromised/asplenic vs 11% in immune-intact patients, with higher peak parasitaemia (2.8% vs 0.9%) and more ARDS (11% vs 4%) and mortality (7% vs 1% at 12 months). PMID 41666444 (TriNetX, 1,566 PCR-confirmed cases, 159 with sepsis; AKI listed among complications; read as abstract only, not selected as it is coded-data and AKI counts are not given in the abstract). | Dogs are mostly immunocompetent hosts infected by different species (B. rossi, B. canis, B. gibsoni, B. vogeli) than B. microti, B. divergens or B. duncani in humans. Human cohort is older adults (mean age 66) with comorbidities; the canine cohorts are not selected by splenic status or immunosuppression. AKI definitions differ (KDIGO-type vs IRIS/azotaemia thresholds); canine retrospective cohorts use admission creatinine and may miss non-azotaemic injury. Treatment differs (human exchange transfusion and atovaquone-azithromycin vs imidocarb/diminazene). Neither dataset isolates renal mechanism. | **speculative** — Both hosts show AKI co-occurring with ARDS and higher mortality in severe babesiosis, but this is a shared clinical association. No study in either host measured a common renal mechanism, and parasitaemia/asplenia as drivers of AKI is demonstrated only at group level in humans and not at all in dogs. |
| PUL | Acute alveolar-capillary injury leading to non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema and ARDS in severe haemoprotozoan disease | [CB111](articles/CB111_2023_lung-pathology-natural-babesia-rossi-infection.md) (2023, B. rossi, necropsy/IHC, n not reported in abstract): fatal natural infection showed alveolar oedema and acute interstitial pneumonia with fibrin and increased macrophages and T cells, partly overlapping exudative diffuse alveolar damage. [CB102](articles/CB102_2022_successful-treatment-suspect-babesia-induced-ards.md) (2022, single puppy, B. canis vogeli): hypoxaemic respiratory failure, suspected ARDS, survived with lung-protective ventilation and neuromuscular blockade. [CB025](articles/CB025_2018_renal-azotemia-associated-clinical-laboratory-findings.md) (2018, B. rossi, n=152): ARDS was associated with renal azotaemia and lower survival. [CB079](articles/CB079_2021_multiple-complications-infected-babesia-gibsoni.md), [CB097](articles/CB097_2022_pathomorphological-changes-german-boxer-infected-babesia.md) and [CB032](articles/CB032_2019_babesiosis-male-boerboel-hematobiochemical-anatomic-pathological.md) are single-animal reports of non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema. [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) (n=6) proposed circulatory failure rather than primary lung injury as the cause of death. Canine ARDS is thus described, but lung injury mechanism was examined only histologically in [CB111](articles/CB111_2023_lung-pathology-natural-babesia-rossi-infection.md), and no study measured lung permeability, BAL or endothelial markers. | PMID 30585748 ([HU016](articles/HU016_2018_babesiosis-cause-acute-respiratory-distress-syndrome.md)) (ICU series): 8 of 22 B. microti ICU patients (36.4%) developed ARDS, 3 of 8 died; SOFA was much higher in non-survivors. PMID 40333147 ([HU015](articles/HU015_2025_pulmonary-manifestations-babesiosis-predictors-mortality-quaternary.md)) (n=147): 42% had respiratory symptoms, 55 had abnormal chest radiographs, and severe dyspnoea, abnormal radiograph, intubation and acute kidney injury were associated with mortality. Neither human babesiosis paper examined lung histology or molecular markers. PMID 31796023 ([HU017](articles/HU017_2019_release-endothelial-activation-markers-lungs-patients.md)) (malaria): post-mortem falciparum lungs with alveolar oedema showed increased VWF and ANG-2 in oedematous alveoli. | Canine data are mostly necropsy and case reports. Human babesiosis data are B. microti in older, often comorbid New York adults, some splenectomised or immunocompromised; the dogs were B. rossi, B. canis, B. gibsoni and B. vogeli. P. falciparum sequesters in the microvasculature, which Babesia does not do to the same extent, so malaria is a comparator, not an equivalent. Human series are retrospective, small and confounded by Lyme coinfection (28% seropositive in PMID 40333147), age and treatment, and the ARDS definition differs between hosts. Canine ARDS is mostly 'suspected' in the digest, with no Berlin-criteria equivalent. | **mechanistically plausible** — The same clinical syndrome (ARDS or non-cardiogenic oedema with respiratory failure, associated with severity and mortality) is documented in both hosts, and canine DAD-like histology (CB111) fits the ARDS concept. However, the alveolar-capillary injury mechanism is not measured in human babesiosis, and the only mechanistic human lung data are from malaria. Clinical ARDS alone does not establish a shared pathway, so the rating stops at plausible. |
| PUL | Pulmonary endothelial activation (VWF, angiopoietin-2) and leukocyte-mediated inflammation as drivers of alveolar permeability | [CB111](articles/CB111_2023_lung-pathology-natural-babesia-rossi-infection.md) (2023, B. rossi, necropsy/IHC): increased pulmonary macrophages and T cells with fibrin and partial DAD overlap. This is the only canine measurement of lung inflammatory cells. No canine study in the digest measured VWF, ANG-2, neutrophil activation or any other endothelial marker in lung, and [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) (n=6) favoured circulatory failure over lung injury. | PMID 31796023 ([HU017](articles/HU017_2019_release-endothelial-activation-markers-lungs-patients.md)) (malaria post-mortem lungs): VWF and ANG-2 increased in oedematous alveoli and intravascular VWF raised in MA-ARDS, with ANG-2-positive leukocytes increased in alveoli of infected patients; the authors read this as Weibel-Palade body release. No human babesiosis paper identified in this search measured endothelial activation in lung. PMID 30585748 ([HU016](articles/HU016_2018_babesiosis-cause-acute-respiratory-distress-syndrome.md)) and PMID 40333147 ([HU015](articles/HU015_2025_pulmonary-manifestations-babesiosis-predictors-mortality-quaternary.md)) report clinical data only. | Evidence is post-mortem, semi-quantitative IHC, with group sizes not stated in the abstract, in P. falciparum (a sequestering parasite). Canine babesiosis, especially B. rossi, is often described as a systemic inflammatory response with haemolysis, and endothelial activation may contribute, but this is not shown in the lung. The canine macrophage and T-cell infiltrate is a different cell readout from the human VWF and ANG-2 findings, so the data are not like-for-like. | **speculative** — The human endothelial-activation evidence is from malaria only, and the canine lung data measure different cell types and no endothelial markers. A shared endothelial mechanism in babesiosis is a hypothesis that fits the clinical picture, not something demonstrated in either babesiosis host. |
| PUL | Pulmonary injury emerging after antiparasitic treatment, possibly linked to parasite killing and release of inflammatory mediators, with kidney injury as a co-occurring severity marker | No canine study in the digest PUL sections reports timing of lung injury relative to antiparasitic therapy. [CB102](articles/CB102_2022_successful-treatment-suspect-babesia-induced-ards.md) (single puppy) is descriptive and does not establish treatment-relative onset or causation. [CB025](articles/CB025_2018_renal-azotemia-associated-clinical-laboratory-findings.md) (n=152, B. rossi) found renal azotaemia associated with ARDS and lower survival, supporting multi-organ co-occurrence but not a treatment-timing effect. | PMID 30585748 ([HU016](articles/HU016_2018_babesiosis-cause-acute-respiratory-distress-syndrome.md)): 6 of 8 babesiosis ARDS cases (75%) began after anti-babesial therapy started, though symptom duration before treatment was longer in that group (7.5 vs 4.5 days, p=0.34), and the authors state the confounding. PMID 40333147 ([HU015](articles/HU015_2025_pulmonary-manifestations-babesiosis-predictors-mortality-quaternary.md)): acute kidney injury and intubation were associated with mortality in the same cohort in which respiratory involvement was common. PMID 31796023 ([HU017](articles/HU017_2019_release-endothelial-activation-markers-lungs-patients.md)) provides no timing information. | Post-treatment pulmonary oedema is well known in severe falciparum malaria but is not demonstrated by the papers selected here (see also PMID 39448997, an alternate not selected, in which 9 of 56 severe falciparum patients developed clinical pulmonary oedema after admission). The human timing signal is retrospective and confounded, and the canine literature has no timing data. Dogs are treated with imidocarb and other regimens, versus atovaquone-azithromycin or clindamycin-quinine in humans, and dogs have no equivalent of exchange-transfusion practice. | **speculative** — The treatment-timing association appears only in a small retrospective human series and is explicitly confounded; no canine study reports it. Renal co-involvement (CB025 and PMID 40333147) is an association, not a pathway demonstrated in both hosts. |
| NEU | Microvascular sequestration and vascular congestion in the brain as a driver of brain swelling | Canine data do not measure brain swelling, brain imaging or brain sequestration. [CB041](articles/CB041_2019_mechanisms-involved-persistence-babesia-canis-infection.md) (2019, B. canis, narrative review largely of the author's own experimental model) proposes that infected erythrocytes sequester in capillaries through endothelial attachment, so that severe disease occurs despite low circulating parasitaemia; this is a hypothesis, not a canine brain measurement. [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) (2021, B. canis, necropsy of 6 dogs) found multi-organ congestion, oedema and effusions and the authors proposed circulatory failure as the cause of death; the digest does not say the brain was examined. [CB116](articles/CB116_2023_experimental-infection-co-infection-chinese-strains.md) (2023, experimental B. vogeli superinfection in E. canis-exposed Beagles, n=18) reported histological lesions in the brain among other organs. [CB069](articles/CB069_2020_elevated-serum-manganese-concentration-possible-predisposing.md) (2020, n=7 dogs per group) described a clinical entity, 'cerebral babesiosis', with raised serum manganese (associational). | PMID 32579667 ([HU021](articles/HU021_2020_parasite-histones-are-toxic-brain-endothelium.md)): in paediatric cerebral malaria, postmortem brain sections showed parasitised erythrocytes sequestered in small vessels, and circulating histone levels were associated with MRI brain swelling. PMID 37703350 ([HU022](articles/HU022_2023_increased-brain-microvascular-hemoglobin-concentrations-children.md)): cerebral microvascular haemoglobin by NIRS was higher in 46 children with cerebral malaria than in uncomplicated malaria and correlated with MRI swelling score (r=0.37), which suggests vascular congestion contributes. PMID 37209667 ([HU023](articles/HU023_2023_neurologic-complications-babesiosis-united-states-2011.md)): more than half of 163 hospitalised babesiosis patients had neurologic symptoms, but no sequestration or imaging data. | The human mechanistic data are from P. falciparum in African children. P. falciparum sequesters through PfEMP1-mediated cytoadherence (EPCR binding, per PMID 34549725, which was read but not selected). Babesia has no PfEMP1; B. canis encodes VESA proteins (103 predicted in CB010), but no canine study here shows brain-specific adherence. Plasmodium schizont rupture is the proposed histone source in PMID 32579667; Babesia replicates by a different division process (general knowledge, not from the retrieved records). The human babesiosis study is of adults, probably B. microti, and many such patients have splenic or comorbid risk factors. Dogs vary in species (B. rossi, B. canis, B. vogeli, B. gibsoni), and the canine 'cerebral babesiosis' definition is clinical. Brain sequestration in dogs has not been shown by the canine papers in the digest. | **mechanistically plausible** — Brain sequestration and congestion causing swelling are demonstrated in human cerebral malaria. In dogs, only a sequestration hypothesis (CB041), non-specific multi-organ congestion (CB080) and brain histological lesions in an experimental model (CB116) are reported, so the same mechanism has not been measured in the canine brain. |
| NEU | Endothelial activation, microthrombosis and blood-brain barrier (BBB) disruption | The canine evidence is systemic, not brain-specific. [CB004](articles/CB004_2017_blood-markers-fibrinolysis-endothelial-activation-babesiosis.md) (2017, B. canis, n=30 dogs vs 10 controls) showed raised thrombomodulin, HMGB-1, VCAM-1 and suPAR, which indicates endothelial activation and altered fibrinolysis. [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md) (2026, B. rossi, n=97 dogs, 12 non-survivors with necropsy) reported platelet activation, factor consumption, fibrinolytic shutdown and fibrin microthrombi as part of thromboinflammation. [CB011](articles/CB011_2017_alteration-haemostatic-parameters-uncomplicated-babesiosis.md) (2017, n=25) found a procoagulant state. None of these assessed BBB permeability, CSF protein or brain endothelium in dogs. | PMID 32579667 ([HU021](articles/HU021_2020_parasite-histones-are-toxic-brain-endothelium.md)): patient sera and purified parasite histones disrupted barrier function in cultured human brain endothelial cells, and luminal vascular histone staining in postmortem cerebral malaria brain colocalised with thrombosis and leakage. Anti-histone antibody and non-anticoagulant heparin blocked the effect in vitro. | The human barrier evidence comes from postmortem tissue and in vitro assays in P. falciparum cerebral malaria. Canine data are blood biomarkers and necropsy fibrin microthrombi from whole-body disease, with no BBB-specific endpoint. A systemic thromboinflammatory state in canine babesiosis (similar to severe malaria or sepsis) does not show that the barrier fails in the brain. Source of histones is unclear in Babesia. Therapeutic inference (heparin) cannot be drawn for dogs from these data. | **mechanistically plausible** — Endothelial activation and microthrombi are measured in both hosts, but only at the systemic level in dogs, and BBB breakdown has been shown only in human cerebral malaria. |
| NEU | Neurologic involvement associated with high parasite burden and concurrent renal failure | [CB025](articles/CB025_2018_renal-azotemia-associated-clinical-laboratory-findings.md) (2018, B. rossi, n=152 dogs, retrospective analysis of three prospective datasets) found renal azotaemia in 14% (21/152), associated with collapse, hypoglycaemia, cerebral babesiosis, ARDS and lower survival (associational). [CB141](articles/CB141_2025_impact-levels-parasitemia-antibodies-acute-phase.md) (2025, B. canis, n=342) found that parasitaemia and CRP correlated positively with haematological severity, but not specifically with neurologic signs. | PMID 37209667 ([HU023](articles/HU023_2023_neurologic-complications-babesiosis-united-states-2011.md)): among 163 hospitalised babesiosis patients, neurologic symptoms (headache, confusion or delirium, impaired consciousness) were associated with high-grade parasitaemia, renal failure and diabetes mellitus. | Both associations are cross-sectional and confounded by overall disease severity; uraemia, hypoglycaemia, hypoxia and metabolic derangements can cause encephalopathy without any parasite-specific brain process. Human cohort is adult, hospitalised, probably B. microti (inference) with comorbidities such as diabetes; canine data are B. rossi with 'cerebral babesiosis' defined clinically. The canine parasitaemia-severity link (CB141) is not neurologic, so the parasite-burden link to neurologic signs is shown only in humans. | **speculative** — A similar association of neurologic signs with renal failure is reported in both hosts, but both are associational, neither isolates a brain-specific mechanism, and the parasite-burden link to neurologic signs is shown only in humans. |
| CAR | Parasite-burden-associated cardiac involvement in severe intraerythrocytic protozoal infection (hypoxic, inflammatory and possibly direct myocardial injury) that is linked to ICU-level illness and death. | [CB014](articles/CB014_2017_cardiac-disorders-infected-babesia-canis.md) (B. canis, 50 dogs vs 20 controls): ECG and echo changes were common but nonspecific, troponin I was raised in 19/50, CK-MB in all, and values were highest in the two dogs that died. [CB146](articles/CB146_2026_cardiac-involvement-european-babesiosis-pathological-perspective.md) (European canine babesiosis necropsies): haemorrhage, pericardial effusion, monocytic leukostasis, oedema, necrosis, congestion and myocarditis. [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) (6 fatal B. canis cases): cardiomyocyte necrosis with multi-organ congestion and oedema. [CB032](articles/CB032_2019_babesiosis-male-boerboel-hematobiochemical-anatomic-pathological.md) (single Boerboel): myocardial haemorrhage. | PMID 35983604 ([HU024](articles/HU024_2022_cardiac-complications-human-babesiosis.md)): 32 of 163 (19.6%) hospitalised babesiosis patients had a cardiac complication (AF, heart failure, QTc prolongation, ischaemia), and these patients had more parasitaemia >10%, longer ICU stay and higher mortality. PMID 33724926 (malaria SR/MA, not selected as a core paper) reports myocarditis and parasitised red cells in the myocardium at autopsy, and cardiac biomarker abnormalities in 0-57% of children. | Human babesiosis data are clinical diagnoses from older adults with comorbidity and frequent splenic or immune compromise (mainly B. microti), with no troponin in the abstract; canine data are mostly young dogs with B. canis or B. rossi and rely on biomarker plus necropsy. The canine histology (myocarditis, necrosis) has no human babesiosis pathology counterpart in the retrieved evidence. Treatment, timing of sampling and survivorship bias (necropsy series include only deaths) differ. Association with high parasitaemia and death is shown in humans, not mechanism. | **mechanistically plausible** — Both hosts show cardiac involvement tracking severity and death, but the human babesiosis paper measures clinical cardiac diagnoses rather than myocardial injury, and the canine paper measures biomarkers and pathology, so the same mechanism was not measured in both. |
| CAR | Troponin release (myocardial injury) that is dissociated from overt systolic pump failure in severe parasitaemic disease. | [CB014](articles/CB014_2017_cardiac-disorders-infected-babesia-canis.md): troponin I raised in 19/50 dogs while ECG/echo abnormalities were described as common but nonspecific; [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md) nonetheless proposes circulatory failure as the probable cause of death with cardiomyocyte necrosis at necropsy. | PMID 29206727 ([HU025](articles/HU025_2017_assessment-myocardial-function-injury-echocardiography-cardiac.md)) (severe falciparum malaria, 104 Ugandan children): troponin I >0.1 ng/mL in 48%, normal median BNP, EF <45% in only 3.8%, high cardiac index in severe malarial anaemia normalising by 24 h, and no correlation between troponin and dysfunction. The authors argue the findings show injury without systolic failure, and that the high cardiac index is an anaemia-driven physiological response. | This is a malaria comparator, not babesiosis. Cohort is children with a high share of severe malarial anaemia, with haemodynamics reflecting FEAST-era transfusion and fluid management; dogs are of mixed age and phenotype. Troponin assays and cut-offs differ between human and canine platforms. P. falciparum cytoadherent microvascular sequestration (including in myocardium) is a distinct mechanism from Babesia; the digest does not show equivalent sequestration in B. canis, though CB146 reports monocytic leukostasis. Both studies are associational and cannot assign cause to the troponin rise. | **mechanistically plausible** — Troponin I and echo or cardiac function were assessed in both hosts and both show injury marker elevation without clear systolic failure, but the canine abstract does not test correlation between troponin and function, and the causal mechanism (hypoxia, anaemia, inflammation, parasitised cells) is not demonstrated in either. |
| CAR | Troponin elevation as a marker of overall illness severity and outcome, rather than an independent prognostic signal, in systemic inflammatory critical illness with circulatory compromise. | [CB014](articles/CB014_2017_cardiac-disorders-infected-babesia-canis.md): troponin I and CK-MB were highest in the two dogs that died (very small number of deaths, no adjustment). [CB080](articles/CB080_2021_disorders-blood-circulation-probable-cause-death.md): circulatory failure proposed as probable cause of death in 6 fatal cases, with congestion, effusions and cardiomyocyte necrosis (descriptive, deaths only). | PMID 39953561 ([HU026](articles/HU026_2025_early-high-sensitivity-troponin-elevation-short.md)) (sepsis, 17 studies, 6242 adults): non-survivors had higher hs-cTn (SMD 0.87) and unadjusted OR 1.78 for mortality, but adjusted OR 1.06 (95% CI 0.99-1.14). PMID 35983604 ([HU024](articles/HU024_2022_cardiac-complications-human-babesiosis.md)) shows cardiac complications in babesiosis cluster with high parasitaemia, ICU stay and death, in line with a severity-marker interpretation. PMID 41666444 (TriNetX, 2026, not selected as a core paper) links babesiosis-related sepsis with shock and higher mortality, though its abstract gives no troponin data. | Sepsis is a heterogeneous syndrome of bacterial and other aetiologies with vasoplegia, and Sepsis-3 populations are adult ICU or ED patients with hs-troponin assays. Canine babesiosis is a haemolytic protozoal disease where anaemia, hypoxia and endothelial or coagulation disturbance (see COA digest) may drive injury. Canine data lack any adjusted prognostic analysis and have only two deaths in CB014, so the sepsis result cannot be transferred. The meta-analysis pools observational studies with substantial heterogeneity in design. | **speculative** — The sepsis finding is a confounded prognostic association in a different disease, and the canine signal rests on two non-survivors without adjustment, so no equivalent analysis exists in dogs and the link is an analogy. |
| MET | Hyperlactataemia and metabolic (lactic) acidosis from tissue hypoperfusion/hypoxia, as a marker of death in a haemolytic, microvascular parasitic disease | [CB074](articles/CB074_2021_prognostic-markers-their-discriminant-score-predicting.md) (B. gibsoni, prognostic case-control, n=108 dogs plus 30 controls): lactate, urea and ALT were specific prognostic markers, and a discriminant score predicted lethal outcome with 79% sensitivity and 83% specificity (survivor/non-survivor split not given in the abstract). [CB068](articles/CB068_2020_acid-base-status-babesiosis-caused-babesia.md) (B. canis, n=14 vs 10 controls): half the dogs had mixed acid-base disorders including lactate/chloride-related acidosis alongside respiratory alkalosis. [CB082](articles/CB082_2021_experimental-babesia-rossi-infection-induces-hemolytic.md) (experimental B. rossi, n=5): acidosis and inflammation reproduced with dose-dependent severity. | PMID 34827078 ([HU018](articles/HU018_2021_alteration-blood-lactate-levels-severe-falciparum.md)): in a meta-analysis of 30 severe falciparum malaria studies (n=30,202), pooled lactate was 5.04 mM, 2.46 mM higher than in uncomplicated malaria (nine studies) and 2.74 mM higher in fatal than surviving cases (six studies; 272 deaths vs 1370 survivors; I2 about 96%). The review itself notes that the prognostic value of lactate still needs study. No human babesiosis study measuring lactate or acid-base status was found. | Human evidence is from P. falciparum malaria (mostly African children and adults), not human babesiosis; sequestration of infected red cells in the microvasculature is a defining mechanism in falciparum malaria and has not been shown for Babesia canis in the same way (CB041 is only a narrative proposal), so the cause of lactate rise may differ (anaemia/hypoxia and hypoperfusion vs obstruction). Canine lactate prognostic data come from B. gibsoni (CB074) while the acid-base descriptions come from B. canis and B. rossi, so no single canine study matches the pooled human contrast. Canine studies are small and associational; the human pooled estimates are very heterogeneous. Splenic status, treatment (artesunate vs imidocarb) and point-of-care lactate methods differ. | **directly demonstrated in both** — The same measurement (blood lactate) and the same contrast (higher in non-survivors than survivors) are reported in dogs with babesiosis (CB074) and in pooled severe malaria data (PMID 34827078). The claim is limited to this prognostic association; a shared causal mechanism for the lactate rise is not shown, and the human data are not from babesiosis. |
| MET | Hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal activation: raised cortisol that tracks severity and discriminates non-survivors | [CB040](articles/CB040_2019_clinical-pathological-description-320-cases-naturally.md) (B. rossi, n=320 PCR-confirmed dogs): many variables were associated with death but none was an independent multivariate predictor; high cortisol (with low T4, high bilirubin and urea) discriminated best. [CB105](articles/CB105_2022_serial-changes-concentrations-cortisol-thyroid-hormones.md) (experimental B. rossi, n=5 Beagles, two inocula): cortisol rose in step with severity and inoculum dose. [CB083](articles/CB083_2021_molecular-characterization-assessment-hematobiochemical-oxidative-indices.md) (B. vogeli, natural infection, group sizes ambiguous): higher plasma cortisol than uninfected dogs, with lower glucose. | PMID 30946764 ([HU019](articles/HU019_2019_cortisol-adrenal-androgens-independent-predictors-mortality.md)): in 139 patients with severe sepsis or septic shock, basal cortisol in the first 24 h was the best mortality predictor (AUC 0.758 in-hospital, 0.759 at 28 days), better than lactate (0.643), SOFA (0.644) and APACHE II (0.618); cortisol above 17.5 ug/dL gave adjusted OR about 10 for in-hospital death. No human babesiosis or malaria cohort selected here reports cortisol as an outcome predictor. | Human comparator is bacterial/viral sepsis in ICU patients, not a parasitic haemolytic infection; the malaria literature (e.g. PMID 37492570, children with severe malaria, higher cortisol in severe than uncomplicated disease with reduced glucocorticoid sensitivity) was read but not selected and is not used as evidence here. Canine cortisol was not an independent predictor in CB040 once multivariable analysis was applied, whereas the human study reports independent associations; canine sampling (single time point, sometimes after supportive care) and assay differ; CB105 has n=5. Dogs and humans differ in cortisol binding and in corticosteroid use before sampling. | **directly demonstrated in both** — Raised cortisol is measured in both hosts and associated with death (CB040 univariate discrimination; PMID 30946764 adjusted OR and AUC), and CB105 shows a severity/dose gradient experimentally. The strength label applies to the association only; canine evidence is weaker (no independent predictor), the human host here is sepsis rather than malaria or babesiosis, and the adrenal mechanism (stress response vs adrenal insufficiency or glucocorticoid resistance) is not tested in dogs. |
| MET | Non-thyroidal illness: low circulating thyroxine with TSH not raised, falling with severity and predicting death | [CB040](articles/CB040_2019_clinical-pathological-description-320-cases-naturally.md) (B. rossi, n=320): low T4 was among the variables that discriminated death best, although no independent multivariate predictor emerged. [CB105](articles/CB105_2022_serial-changes-concentrations-cortisol-thyroid-hormones.md) (experimental B. rossi, n=5): T4 fell with severity and inoculum dose while TSH stayed within reference limits. A B. canis thyroid study (n=60) was not used because only its title was accessible. | PMID 33652253 ([HU020](articles/HU020_2021_thyroid-hormone-levels-predictor-marker-predict.md)): in 929 septic patients (MIMIC-III), T4 was significantly lower in those with unfavourable outcome (P<0.01); lower admission T4 carried OR 0.556 (95% CI 0.41-0.75) for poor outcome, and a combined fT4, T4 and SAPS II model reached AUROC 0.82 for ICU survival, above SAPS II alone. No human babesiosis study of thyroid hormones was found. | Human evidence is from sepsis (retrospective database analysis with unmeasured selection of who had thyroid tests), not babesiosis or malaria; where malaria has been examined (PMID 38270243, uncomplicated paediatric falciparum malaria, n=90 cases) free T3 was raised rather than lowered, with fT4 unchanged, so the direction of thyroid change is not uniform across parasitic diseases and was not selected as evidence. Canine studies measured total T4 (the digest does not say which fractions) and the dog is a species in which non-thyroidal illness effects on T4 are well documented but with different binding proteins; canine data include an experimental model of only five dogs. Neither CB040 nor the human study shows that low T4 is causal rather than a marker of illness severity. | **directly demonstrated in both** — The same analyte (T4) is lower in more severe or fatal disease in dogs with babesiosis (CB040, CB105) and in septic patients (PMID 33652253), and the TSH-unchanged feature is reported in CB105 (the human study abstract does not report TSH). The finding is an association with severity/outcome only, and the human host is a sepsis comparator, not a haemoparasitic infection. |
| SEV | Loss of splenic function (asplenia or splenectomy) removes the main site of clearance of parasitised erythrocytes, which gives high parasitaemia, severe haemolytic disease and prolonged or relapsing infection. | [CB021](articles/CB021_2018_first-description-pathogenicity-babesia-vogeli-experimentally.md) (B. vogeli, experimental, n=6): intact Beagles had mild disease then prolonged subclinical infection, while splenectomised dogs developed severe, life-threatening anaemia and thrombocytopenia. [CB120](articles/CB120_2023_case-report-fatal-babesia-vulpes-infection.md) (single case): a splenectomised, immunosuppressed dog with haemotropic Mycoplasma co-infection developed severe B. vulpes disease and was euthanised. [CB116](articles/CB116_2023_experimental-infection-co-infection-chinese-strains.md) (B. vogeli superinfection in E. canis-exposed Beagles): splenectomy raised E. canis loads. [CB087](articles/CB087_2021_morphofunctional-changes-spleen-components-pathogenetic-substantiation.md) (n=187 infected dogs vs 15 controls): histology showed splenomegaly, rupture and circulatory disorder, which describes splenic involvement but not function. The canine experiments are small, and the other canine items are a case report and a descriptive series. | 39873391: patients with asplenia/hyposplenia or immunocompromise (n=112 of 225) had higher peak parasitaemia (2.8% vs 0.9%), more AKI (24% vs 11%), more ARDS (11% vs 4%), longer treatment (median 43 days with both immunocompromise and asplenia), and 12-month mortality 7% vs 1%. 28722598: asplenia or immunocompromise predicted severe infection, hospitalisation and prolonged therapy (P<0.01, n=128). | The human data are retrospective associations in older, mostly northeastern US adults infected with B. microti. Asplenia/hyposplenia is pooled with immunocompromise in 39873391, so the spleen effect cannot be isolated. Canine splenectomy is a controlled experimental exposure in healthy young Beagles with B. vogeli, a low-virulence species. Natural canine splenic disease is mostly spleen enlargement rather than absence. B. rossi and B. canis are virulent in intact dogs, so spleen loss is not needed for severe canine disease, whereas B. microti is usually mild in immunocompetent humans. Mechanism (splenic filtration) is inferred in both hosts and was not directly measured in these papers. | **directly demonstrated in both** — The same exposure (no functional spleen) and the same outcome class (severe disease or high burden) are measured in both hosts, and the canine data are experimental. This is demonstrated for the exposure-outcome relationship, not for the splenic mechanism itself, which is inferred. |
| SEV | Impaired adaptive immunity (T-cell or B-cell deficiency, immunosuppressive therapy) leads to uncontrolled parasitaemia, severe disease and relapse. | [CB008](articles/CB008_2017_flow-cytometric-assessment-lymphocyte-immunophenotypes-naturally.md) (B. rossi, n=44 infected, 5 controls): complicated cases had lower CD3+ and CD4+ lymphocyte percentages than controls and uncomplicated dogs, which the authors read as functional immune suppression. [CB120](articles/CB120_2023_case-report-fatal-babesia-vulpes-infection.md) (single case): a splenectomised, immunosuppressed dog with B. vulpes died. [CB052](articles/CB052_2020_long-term-follow-up-owned-free.md) (B. rossi, endemic free-roaming dogs): 31% of 109 dogs were seropositive but healthy, and the confirmed cases were mostly mild, consistent with acquired immunity. [CB141](articles/CB141_2025_impact-levels-parasitemia-antibodies-acute-phase.md) (B. canis, n=342): antibody level correlated inversely with haematological severity, and autochthonous dogs were more severe and immunologically naive. None of these studies tested drug-induced immunosuppression or B-cell depletion in dogs. | 39873391: immunocompromised or asplenic patients had higher peak parasitaemia, higher 12-month mortality and recurrence (8% vs 0%); haematological malignancy (OR 7.0) and B-cell-depleting therapy (OR 9.4) predicted recurrence. 28722598: immunocompromising conditions predicted severe infection and prolonged therapy. | Canine immune-deficit data are associational: lymphocyte subsets in sick dogs, or antibody in naive vs exposed dogs. They do not test iatrogenic B-cell depletion, haematological malignancy or transplant, which drive the human findings. Canine lymphopenia during acute babesiosis may be a consequence of severe inflammation rather than a pre-existing susceptibility. Human relapse follows prolonged persistence under B-cell depletion, while canine relapse data here come mainly from B. gibsoni, which is not cleared by treatment (CB094, CB067). The human cohorts also have comorbidity and polypharmacy confounding. | **mechanistically plausible** — Both hosts link weaker adaptive immunity to worse outcomes, but the canine evidence is indirect (CD4 depletion in complicated disease, antibody inversely related to severity, one immunosuppressed case) and no canine study measures the human exposure (B-cell depletion) or the recurrence endpoint. |
| SEV | Parasite burden relates to the degree of haemolysis and the severity grade; high parasitaemia marks the severe, organ-dysfunction end of the spectrum. | [CB141](articles/CB141_2025_impact-levels-parasitemia-antibodies-acute-phase.md) (B. canis, n=342, subsets with parasitaemia data): parasitaemia and CRP correlated positively with haematological severity. [CB123](articles/CB123_2024_age-sex-breed-effect-laboratory-parameters.md) (B. canis, n=61): young dogs had higher parasitaemia and more severe anaemia. [CB073](articles/CB073_2021_systemic-inflammatory-response-syndrome-naturally-infected.md) (B. canis, n=54): parasite load and acute-phase proteins were not independent associates of SIRS, while leukopenia and monocytopenia were. So the canine association is clear for anaemia but not for the systemic inflammatory endpoint. | 40333147: 75% of the 63 severe cases had parasitaemia of 10% or more, and haemoglobin of 8 g/dL or lower was associated with severe disease. 39873391: patients with higher complication rates (AKI, ARDS, transfusion) also had higher peak parasitaemia (2.8% vs 0.9%). | Parasitaemia is read from a smear in both hosts but is not standardised across them. Human parasitaemia thresholds (5% and 10%) are used clinically for exchange transfusion decisions, so selection bias and treatment effects enter the human data. Canine severity is graded as complicated vs uncomplicated or by MODS and SIRS, which is a different scale. B. rossi and B. canis hit very high parasitaemia less often than B. microti does in splenectomised hosts; B. rossi severity partly reflects an inflammatory response out of proportion to burden. Sequestration and microthrombosis (shown in B. rossi, CB145) differ from the human B. microti picture, and P. falciparum-type cytoadherence does not apply to either Babesia. | **directly demonstrated in both** — Parasitaemia and a severity outcome are measured and found to be positively associated in both hosts. This is rated with caution because the canine evidence is mixed (CB073 negative for SIRS), the canine severity endpoint in CB141 is haematological, and the human data are unadjusted descriptive associations. |
| SEV | Acute kidney injury and respiratory failure (ARDS, pulmonary oedema) as organ-dysfunction endpoints that mark and predict death. | [CB025](articles/CB025_2018_renal-azotemia-associated-clinical-laboratory-findings.md) (B. rossi, n=152): renal azotaemia in 14% was associated with collapse, hypoglycaemia, cerebral babesiosis, ARDS and lower survival, and was often missed by urine specific gravity. [CB061](articles/CB061_2020_complications-risk-factors-regarding-outcomes-babesiosis.md) (B. canis, n=240): creatinine >5 mg/dL, phosphate >3 mmol/L and albumin <2.2 g/dL were associated with death, with 33.9% mortality when the kidney was involved. [CB043](articles/CB043_2019_clinical-occurrence-babesiosis-coastal-area-black.md) (smear-positive dogs, n=85): MODS in 15.3% with recovery of 38.4% vs 81.6% for uncomplicated disease. [CB074](articles/CB074_2021_prognostic-markers-their-discriminant-score-predicting.md) (B. gibsoni): urea, ALT and lactate predicted death. [CB032](articles/CB032_2019_babesiosis-male-boerboel-hematobiochemical-anatomic-pathological.md) and [CB097](articles/CB097_2022_pathomorphological-changes-german-boxer-infected-babesia.md) (single necropsies): pulmonary oedema and glomerular lesions. [CB040](articles/CB040_2019_clinical-pathological-description-320-cases-naturally.md) (B. rossi, n=320): no independent multivariate predictor emerged. | 40333147: mortality was higher with age >=70, encephalopathy, AKI, severe dyspnoea, abnormal chest radiograph and intubation (10 deaths in 147); creatinine rise and radiograph abnormality were associated with severe disease; respiratory symptoms in 42%. 39873391: AKI 24% vs 11% and ARDS 11% vs 4% in immunocompromised/asplenic vs immune-intact patients. | Canine kidney injury in B. rossi and B. canis includes documented glomerular and tubular injury (CB001, CB053) and haemoglobinuria. Human data are mostly clinical AKI without histology. Canine mortality is partly euthanasia-driven, whereas human deaths follow ICU care. Human cohorts are older (median ~66-71 y) with cardiac and renal comorbidity, which dogs lack. The human mortality predictors come from 10 events and are unlikely to be independent, similar to CB040, where univariable associations did not survive multivariate analysis. | **directly demonstrated in both** — The same organ-dysfunction endpoints (renal injury, pulmonary failure) are measured and tied to severity and death in both hosts. Evidence strength is limited by retrospective design, small event numbers in the human cohort, and unadjusted associations in several canine studies. |
| SEV | Haemolysis and hepatic/biochemical markers (bilirubin, anaemia depth) as indicators of severity. | [CB040](articles/CB040_2019_clinical-pathological-description-320-cases-naturally.md) (B. rossi, n=320): high bilirubin, urea, cortisol and low T4 discriminated best for death, although no independent multivariate predictors emerged. [CB099](articles/CB099_2022_occurrence-babesiosis-rhine-main-area-hesse.md) (n=81): 90% hyperbilirubinaemic, 82% anaemic. [CB074](articles/CB074_2021_prognostic-markers-their-discriminant-score-predicting.md) (B. gibsoni): urea, ALT and lactate were prognostic. [CB067](articles/CB067_2020_analysis-risk-factors-associated-recurrence-babesiosis.md) (B. gibsoni, n=145): RBC <2x10^6/ul and persistent anaemia raised recurrence risk. | 28722598: hyperbilirubinaemia, nausea/vomiting and diarrhoea predicted severe infection, hospitalisation and prolonged therapy (P<0.01). 40333147: haemoglobin of 8 g/dL or lower was associated with severe disease. | Bilirubin in dogs reflects haemolysis, cholestasis and hepatic injury and has no established cutoff comparable to the human series. The human bilirubin finding is univariable with P values only in a small 2017 cohort. Canine marker panels include lactate, cortisol and thyroid hormones, which none of the human papers tested. Secondary IMHA (CB067, CB153) has a human counterpart only as post-babesiosis autoimmune haemolytic anaemia, not covered by these papers. | **mechanistically plausible** — Haemolysis and bilirubin markers are measured in both hosts, but the evidence is univariable and not independently confirmed in either (CB040 found no independent predictor, and 28722598 gives only P values), so the shared prognostic utility is plausible rather than demonstrated. |
| SEV | Age and co-infection as modifiers of severity. | [CB043](articles/CB043_2019_clinical-occurrence-babesiosis-coastal-area-black.md): older age associated with severe disease (MODS). [CB123](articles/CB123_2024_age-sex-breed-effect-laboratory-parameters.md) (B. canis, n=61): young dogs had more severe anaemia and leukopenia and higher parasitaemia, which is the opposite direction. [CB060](articles/CB060_2020_clinicopathological-molecular-profiles-babesia-vogeli-infection.md) (B. vogeli +/- E. canis, n=44): co-infected dogs had lower RBC, Hb and Hct. [CB116](articles/CB116_2023_experimental-infection-co-infection-chinese-strains.md) (experimental): B. vogeli superinfection in E. canis-exposed Beagles gave worse anaemia and thrombocytopenia. [CB126](articles/CB126_2024_prevalence-co-infections-ehrlichia-spp-or.md) (B. rossi, n=150): no haematological variable differed with E. canis co-infection (only 3 co-infected). [CB109](articles/CB109_2023_haematological-indices-immune-response-profiles-naturally.md): co-infected dogs had milder ALP, urea and creatinine changes. | 40333147: age >=70 was associated with severe disease and with death. 28722598: concurrent Lyme disease did not worsen severity and was associated with less hospitalisation. | Human risk concentrates in older adults with comorbidity and immune senescence. Canine age effects are inconsistent between studies and confounded by breed and naive immune status in young dogs. Human co-infection is with Borrelia, which is transmitted by the same Ixodes tick, while canine co-infections are with other haemoparasites (E. canis, D. repens, Hepatozoon). Canine co-infection results are mixed (CB060, CB116 worse; CB126, CB109 no change or milder), and the human Lyme data are a single-cohort finding. | **speculative** — Canine age and co-infection findings point in conflicting directions, the co-infecting organisms are not comparable, and the human evidence is one cohort association each. There is no shared mechanism that has been measured in both hosts. |

## Research questions

Five research questions that link the pathophysiology priorities in the category syntheses (ERY, INF, END, COA, REN, PUL, NEU, CAR, MET, OXO, SEV; 2006–2026) to the verified datasets in [datasets.md](datasets.md), or that state which data are still needed. Links are relative to the review root.

**Ground rules for every question**

- These are questions, not findings. None of the analyses below has been run. No result, effect direction or "subtype" is promised.
- "Verified" means status 1 in [datasets.jsonl](datasets.jsonl), a public download that was checked. Request-only records (RQ-…) are unverified and may never be released.
- Every verified dataset is small for its number of features, so ordinary statistics come first. Machine learning is mentioned only where it is not ruled out, and then only as penalised regression with animal-level resampling.
- Novelty was checked against PubMed on 2026-10-23 with NCBI E-utilities (esearch, then efetch abstracts). Only abstracts were read, so full texts could still hold relevant analyses. On 2026-10-28 the labels were re-checked against the 2006–2026 included set, without a new PubMed search; where an included 2006–2016 paper covers part of a question, the label says so and cites it. PubMed hits that are now included articles carry their IDs.
- Cohort overlaps follow [cohorts.json](cohorts.json). Papers from the same cohort, or datasets from the same source (S-[CB517](articles/CB517_2016_excessive-pro-inflammatory-serum-cytokine-concentrations.md) and S-[CB050](articles/CB050_2020_neutrophil-myeloperoxidase-index-babesiosis-caused-babesia.md), SRC-A), are not independent replication.

---

### Q1. Haemolysis, free haem, endothelial activation and tubular injury in large-*Babesia* infection

**Hypothesis.** In dogs naturally infected with *B. rossi* or *B. canis*, plasma cell-free haemoglobin (CFH) at admission, and depletion of its scavengers haptoglobin and haemopexin, is associated with endothelial activation (angiopoietin-2, syndecan-1, VCAM-1) and with urinary tubular injury markers (uNGAL and KIM-1, each normalised to urine creatinine). The association is expected to hold after adjustment for haematocrit, parasitaemia, lactate and mean arterial pressure (MAP). Serum urea is not a renal outcome here, because the raised urea of babesiosis is mostly not renal ([CB325](articles/CB325_2006_elevated-serum-urea-creatinine-ratio-babesiosis.md)).

There is a stated counter-hypothesis. In healthy dogs, experimental haemoglobinaemia alone did not cause nephropathy (Lobetti 1996, PMID 9284030 ([BG002](articles/BG002_1996_comparative-role-haemoglobinaemia-hypoxia-development-babesial.md))). In the CH29 dogs, induced haemoglobinaemia did not reproduce the raised urea:creatinine ratio of babesiosis, and GFR was normal ([CB412](articles/CB412_2011_changes-serum-urea-creatinine-ratio-babesiosis.md)). Any CFH association may therefore depend on co-factors such as hypoxia, hypotension, inflammation or acidic, methaemoglobin-rich urine, rather than on a direct effect of CFH.

**Supporting canine papers**

- Tubular and glomerular injury before azotaemia:
  - *B. rossi*: urinary IgG, CRP and RBP rose while serum creatinine did not in [CB435](articles/CB435_2012_assessment-renal-dysfunction-using-urinary-markers.md) (2012, CH36), so older work already shows this signal; [CB053](articles/CB053_2020_evaluation-acute-kidney-injury-complicated-or.md) adds NGAL.
  - *B. canis*: [CB024](articles/CB024_2018_glomerular-tubular-kidney-damage-markers-babesiosis.md).
  - Clustering of renal azotaemia with ARDS, cerebral signs and death: [CB025](articles/CB025_2018_renal-azotemia-associated-clinical-laboratory-findings.md).
- Tissue: [CB341](articles/CB341_2007_histological-ultrastructural-studies-renal-lesions-babesia.md) (2007, 8 fatal *B. canis* cases): tubular necrosis and capillary endothelial damage, attributed to hypoxia; 7 dogs had imidocarb.
- Raised urea with creatinine and cystatin-C in range: [CB325](articles/CB325_2006_elevated-serum-urea-creatinine-ratio-babesiosis.md) and [CB412](articles/CB412_2011_changes-serum-urea-creatinine-ratio-babesiosis.md) (CH29, one source).
- Endothelial activation:
  - [CB446](articles/CB446_2013_markers-coagulation-activation-endothelial-stimulation-inflammation.md) (2013, 12 *B. canis* dogs): soluble ICAM-1 raised, von Willebrand factor activity lowered.
  - [CB004](articles/CB004_2017_blood-markers-fibrinolysis-endothelial-activation-babesiosis.md): VCAM-1 separated complicated from uncomplicated cases.
  - [CB145](articles/CB145_2026_thromboinflammation-virulent-babesiosis.md): fibrin microthrombi in several organs of non-survivors.
- Hypotension: MAP below 80 mmHg in 7 of 48 dogs, with pressure inversely related to creatinine ([CB463](articles/CB463_2014_association-between-decreased-blood-pressure-azotaemia.md)); TNF-α tracked low pressure in [CB473](articles/CB473_2014_increased-concentration-serum-tnf-alpha-correlations.md) (one Warsaw group).
- Scavenger proteins point in different directions:
  - Haptoglobin was low in [CB058](articles/CB058_2020_serum-amyloid-haptoglobin-ceruloplasmin-albumin-levels.md) and rose then fell after treatment in [CB323](articles/CB323_2006_evidence-acute-phase-response-naturally-infected.md).
  - Haemopexin was higher in [CB117](articles/CB117_2023_proteomics-identification-overexpressed-serum-proteins-babesiacanis.md) (3 vs 3 dogs).
